# ![[Uncaptioned image]](2608.05141v1/Assets/Squid.png)  OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling

Indraneil Paul Affiliation: UKP Lab & Center for Applied Cybersecurity
ATHENE, TU Darmstadt    Falko Helm Affiliation: UKP Lab & Center for
Applied Cybersecurity ATHENE, TU Darmstadt    Goran Glavaš Affiliation:
CAIDAS, University of Würzburg    Iryna Gurevych Affiliation: UKP Lab &
Center for Applied Cybersecurity ATHENE, TU Darmstadt

###### Abstract

Context lengths of language models (LMs) have dramatically increased,
driven by the demands for in-context learning, self-improvement, and
long-horizon agentic workflows. Existing long-context corpora, however,
are dominated by books, academic articles, and code repositories, which
are finite resources and often scarce in long-distance dependencies. In
this work, we introduce OctoLong, a context engineering pipeline that
instruments an AST parser, a language server backend, and a package
manager to facilitate the recursive retrieval of code references,
enabling the curation of dependency-rich code contexts up to millions of
tokens in length. We then train OctoLong-Instruct, a suite of capable
long-context open LMs, derived from base models ranging in size from
600M to 14B parameters, via context-extension mid-training on a
$`\approx`$50B-token mixture containing $`\approx`$6.2B tokens of
OctoLong code contexts, followed by $`\approx`$10B tokens of instruction
tuning. Our training ablations and experimental evaluations against 18
strong long-context open-weight LMs show that supplanting just 12% of
conventional context-extension corpora with OctoLong data yields
substantial gains in long-range retrieval, long-term state tracking,
repository-level code understanding, and downstream agentic tasks, while
also enhancing API usage in short-context coding scenarios.

## 1 Introduction

Emergent language model (LM) applications such as in-context learning
([Bertsch et al., 2025](#bib.bib152)), retrieval-augmented generation
([Qian et al., 2025](#bib.bib153); [Kang et al., 2025](#bib.bib156)),
and agentic tool-use ([Merrill et al., 2026](#bib.bib154); [Jimenez et
al., 2024](#bib.bib155)) demand long-horizon capabilities ([Kwa et al.,
2025](#bib.bib140)), exponentially driving up the demand on the context
lengths the LMs are expected to support (ref.
[Figure 1](#S1.F1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).
Prior attempts to extend the context length mainly involve architectural
interventions such as sparse attention ([Yuan et al.,
2025](#bib.bib157); [Han et al., 2024](#bib.bib158); [Beltagy et al.,
2020](#bib.bib159)) and linear attention ([Hatamizadeh et al.,
2026](#bib.bib160); [Dao and Gu, 2024](#bib.bib161)). In isolation,
these approaches deteriorate the performance on longer contexts ([Lu et
al., 2024](#bib.bib164); [Huang, 2024](#bib.bib165)), causing LM
developers to turn to orthogonal axes of improvement such as
hardware-use improvements ([Li et al., 2023a](#bib.bib166); [Liu et al.,
2023](#bib.bib167)), inference-time context management ([Li et al.,
2024c](#bib.bib162); [Zhang et al., 2023b](#bib.bib163)), and better
data engineering ([Li et al., 2025b](#bib.bib76)).

![Refer to caption](2608.05141v1/ContextTrend.png)

Figure 1: Context lengths of open-weight LMs. The trend is exponential,
doubling on average every 5 months.

Data Engineering for Context Extension. Data engineering for
long-context training ([Fu et al., 2024](#bib.bib5)) revolves around
engineering long text inputs with a high density of referrals (i.e.,
dependencies), spanning a range of distances. Existing approaches span
the gamut of token-level dependency filtering ([Deng et al.,
2025](#bib.bib40); [Rathi and Radford, 2026](#bib.bib171)), curating
multiple salient documents in context ([Gao et al., 2025b](#bib.bib15);
[Jia et al., 2025](#bib.bib172); [Li et al., 2024a](#bib.bib173)), and
referral-based packing ([Zhuang et al., 2025](#bib.bib174);
[Staniszewski et al., 2025](#bib.bib175)). Practically, however, the
contexts engineered by such approaches often lack complex multi-hop
dependencies ([Li et al., 2024b](#bib.bib176)) and mine irrelevant or
contradictory information ([Du et al., 2025b](#bib.bib147); [Tang et
al., 2025](#bib.bib177)). In this work, we introduce cross-repository
code dependency mining. This referral packing technique taps into openly
available code data to unlock a virtually limitless source of multi-hop
referral-dense contexts that transcend repository boundaries.

Contributions. We address the abovementioned research gaps and make the
following contributions:

We introduce OctoLong, a pipeline to generate cross-repository code
contexts using an AST parser, an LSP server, and a package manager in
tandem. Analyses of attention maps and model likelihoods show that our
pipeline produces exceptionally dependency-rich long contexts
([Section 3](#S3 "3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).

We collect $`\approx`$6.2B tokens of cross-repository long-context data
with the OctoLong pipeline and combine it with other long-data sources
to curate the $`\approx`$50B-token OctoLong-LCFT corpus. We then compile
the $`\approx`$10B-token OctoLong-SFT mix of open-source instruction and
agentic data
([Section 4](#S4 "4 OctoLong-Instruct Models ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).

Using the above corpora, we subject Qwen3 dense base models to
long-context fine-tuning (LCFT), quadrupling their supported context
length to 128K, followed by supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to obtain the
OctoLong-Instruct suite
([Section 4](#S4 "4 OctoLong-Instruct Models ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).

We establish, via extensive experiments and ablations, that LCFT with
our OctoLong data in the long-context data mix leads to substantial
improvements not only in long-context code tasks but also in
general-domain long-context applications including agentic use-cases,
without penalty on short-context LM capabilities
([Section 5](#S5 "5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).

![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzIucDEucGljMiIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEzLjg0IiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgc3R5bGU9InZlcnRpY2FsLWFsaWduOi0yLjQ2cHgiIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgdmlld2JveD0iMCAwIDEzLjg0IDEzLjg0IiB3aWR0aD0iMTMuODQiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7IiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC40cHQiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMTMuODQpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSg2LjkyLDApIHRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDYuOTIpIj48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDQuMTUgNi45MiBMIC00LjE1IDYuOTIgQyAtNS42OCA2LjkyIC02LjkyIDUuNjggLTYuOTIgNC4xNSBMIC02LjkyIC00LjE1IEMgLTYuOTIgLTUuNjggLTUuNjggLTYuOTIgLTQuMTUgLTYuOTIgTCA0LjE1IC02LjkyIEMgNS42OCAtNi45MiA2LjkyIC01LjY4IDYuOTIgLTQuMTUgTCA2LjkyIDQuMTUgQyA2LjkyIDUuNjggNS42OCA2LjkyIDQuMTUgNi45MiBaIE0gLTYuOTIgLTYuOTIiIC8+PC9nPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6I0ZGRkZGRjstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNGRkZGRkY7IiBmaWxsPSIjRkZGRkZGIiBzdHJva2U9IiNGRkZGRkYiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCAtMy45OCAtNC40NikiPjxmb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0IHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1mby13aWR0aDowLjVlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6MC41NmVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWRlcHRoOjBlbTtmb250LXNpemU6MTEuNXB0OyIgaGVpZ2h0PSI4LjkyIiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCA4LjkyKSIgd2lkdGg9IjcuOTYiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250YWluZXIiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250ZW50Ij48c3BhbiBpZD0iUzIucDEucGljMi4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3RleHQgbHR4X2ZvbnRfYm9sZCIgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZnLWNvbG9yOiNGRkZGRkY7Ij4yPC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L3NwYW4+PC9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0PjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)
long-context code data mining.

Cross-Document Context Engineering. Prior work towards engineering
cross-document contexts have relied on hyperlinks ([Zhuang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib174); [Yasunaga et al., 2022](#bib.bib185)), book BISAC
codes ([Jia et al., 2026](#bib.bib186)), event co-references ([Caciularu
et al., 2021](#bib.bib187)), and repository-level code dependencies [Guo
et al. (2024)](#bib.bib33). These approaches are all either hard to
scale or yield loosely related contexts. In contrast, we detail how to
recursively mine referred class and function implementations beyond
repository boundaries — an untapped source of tightly-coupled data whose
acquisition is easy to scale.

Mining Long Self-Contained Code Data. Early file-level attempts to mine
long code data found that long code files are usually low-quality
configuration files and uninformative build artifacts ([Singh et al.,
2024](#bib.bib168)). Subsequent methods have since moved on to
serializing code at the repository level ([Ding et al.,
2024](#bib.bib169); [Deng et al., 2024](#bib.bib170)). Serialized
repositories have since become the anchor of mid-training ([Liu et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib179); [Blakeney et al., 2024](#bib.bib180)) and
context-extension ([Mo et al., 2025](#bib.bib178)) corpora. However,
most code is not self-contained at the repository level ([Yu et al.,
2024](#bib.bib181)), which restricts models’ ability to reason about
multi-repository projects ([Chen et al., 2026](#bib.bib182)). Attempts
to train on cross-repository contexts are stymied by inadequate tooling
([Usai et al., 2026](#bib.bib183)) and have thus been limited to
sourcing dependency library names ([Liao et al., 2024](#bib.bib184)). In
[Section 3](#S3 "3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling"),
we detail a novel pipeline to source referred implementations beyond
repository bounds.

## 3 The OctoLong Dataset

Sourcing long-context code for language modeling has traditionally
focused on serializing individual code repositories in dependency
traversal order ([Lozhkov et al., 2024](#bib.bib32); [Guo et al.,
2024](#bib.bib33); [Zhang et al., 2023a](#bib.bib34); [Shrivastava et
al., 2023](#bib.bib35); [Sapronov and Glukhov, 2025](#bib.bib36)). To
test our hypotheses from
[Section 1](#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling"),
we develop the OctoLong pipeline to retrieve code dependencies across
repository boundaries.

Language Server Protocol. We leverage the Language Server Protocol
(LSP),¹¹ 1 [![[Uncaptioned image]](2608.05141v1/Assets/Microsoft.png)
Language Server
Protocol](https://microsoft.github.io/language-server-protocol/) a
JSON-RPC standard that provides a unified language- and client-agnostic
wire interface to source code analyses by fronting language-specific
compilers. Crucial for our purposes, mature language server
implementations for popular programming languages can use the
established conventions of detected package managers, in tandem with the
abstract syntax tree (AST) information produced by the compiler, to map
the location of imported classes and functions. Traditionally used by
IDEs, the LSP ecosystem offers a robust, type- and scope-aware approach
to retrieve dependency code unattainable by predecessors reliant on pure
syntactic parsing.²² 2 ![[Uncaptioned
image]](2608.05141v1/Assets/Git.png) [universal-ctags/ctags](https://github.com/universal-ctags/ctags)

Figure 2: Overview of the OctoLong pipeline’s core stages. Utilizing AST
information, a language server backend leverages package manager
metadata to recursively retrieve dependency data and engineer very long
code contexts.

OctoLong Data Acquisition.
[Figure 2](#S3.F2 "In 3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
illustrates all stages of OctoLong data acquisition pipeline for long
code contexts. We start by sourcing from SEART Hub ([Dabic et al.,
2024](#bib.bib37)) reputable and actively maintained Python
repositories³³ 3 While our data collection is restricted to Python, the
OctoLong pipeline is extensible to any programming language with a
mature LSP backend and a package manager., with at least 15 GitHub stars
and more than 5 contributors. From these, we retain only those that (a)
declare dependencies and (b) are installable, resulting in 12K
repositories. Each Python file from retained repositories becomes a
candidate seed: we keep the 92K seeds that pass the OpenCoder’s default
code-quality filtering pipeline ([Huang et al., 2025](#bib.bib38)).
Subsequently, we provision each seed in a containerized sandbox with its
repository and dependency environment and traverse the dependency graph
via breadth-first search (BFS). We traverse up to a pre-specified depth
of 10 steps or when we collect 128K tokens of code (whichever is
fulfilled first), prepending the code retrieved in each step to the
context. Each hop consists of a tree-sitter⁴⁴ 4 ![[Uncaptioned
image]](2608.05141v1/Assets/Git.png) [tree-sitter/tree-sitter](https://github.com/tree-sitter/tree-sitter)
query that extracts class and function call-sites, followed by a
GoToDeclaration call⁵⁵ 5 While the LSP standard defines a
GoToImplementation endpoint, which would significantly simplify our
collection, this remains unsupported by most language servers. per
call-site using the jedi ⁶⁶ 6 ![[Uncaptioned
image]](2608.05141v1/Assets/Git.png) [pappasam/jedi-language-server](https://github.com/pappasam/jedi-language-server)
language server; this is followed by another tree-sitter query that
obtains the function or class boundaries and extracts the implementation
from the target file. We make sure never to repeat classes and
functions. We also discard contexts shallower than 5 levels as well as
those with a hop success rate below 60%. Following this procedure, we
end up with $`\approx`$6.2B code tokens.

Table 1: Comparison of the cross-repository contexts from the OctoLong
pipeline (configured with varying depth limits) against existing
long-context data sources. OctoLong flexibly sources long-context data
that are (i) orders of magnitude longer and (ii) exhibit superior
dependency density compared to existing long-context sources.

Comparison of Long Context Data Sources. Our goal is to test our
hypothesis that cross-repository code contexts constitute a very good
source of long and dependency-rich data. To this end, we additionally
acquire preprocessed long-context corpora from a variety of traditional
sources, including books, code repositories, citation graphs, and
retrieved documents, and compare them against our data sourced via the
OctoLong pipeline without token length restrictions, using BFS depth
limits of 8, 10, and 15, respectively. A comparison of context length
distributions
[Table 1](#S3.T1 "In 3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
shows that our OctoLong pipeline produces contexts that surpass those in
any existing source by orders of magnitude.

Further, we compare the density, uniformity, and salience of the
dependencies within the long-context data sources. This comparison is
critical, as training on dependency-deprived long contexts offers
limited performance gains over short-context language modeling ([Chen et
al., 2024](#bib.bib39); [Deng et al., 2025](#bib.bib40)), while
substantially increasing training costs. We inspect all long-context
corpora in
[Table 1](#S3.T1 "In 3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
with a modified LongAttn ([Wu et al., 2025b](#bib.bib17)) score (the
formula below). Specifically, we use the first attention layer of a Phi3
model ([Microsoft, 2024](#bib.bib41))⁷⁷ 7 For context spans longer than
128K tokens, we compute dependency saliency metrics on the trailing 128K
tokens. to calculate the mean attention mass $`M`$ of the last 2K query
tokens against the most recent eighth, quarter, and half of the
$`(n-2)`$K token keys from the prefix. We average these as follows
(denoted with (Dep. Mean in
[Table 1](#S3.T1 "In 3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")):

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\begin{gathered}\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{Dep.}}=\frac{1}{6K}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\;\sum_{q=(n-2)K+1}^{nK}\;\sum_{i=b_{j}}^{(n-2)K}M_{i,q}$}\\
\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\text{where}\;\;b_{j}=(n-2)K-c_{j}+1,\ \ c_{j}=\frac{(n-2)K}{2^{j}},\ \ j\in\{1,2,3\}$}\end{gathered}
``` |  |

We also track the mean attention mass fraction the final 2K tokens allot
to the first four sink positions (Sink Prop. in
[Table 1](#S3.T1 "In 3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).
These metrics, in tandem, test for data-induced lost-middle patterns
([Salvatore et al., 2025](#bib.bib42); [Liu et al., 2024b](#bib.bib7))
where recency ([Baddeley and Hitch, 1993](#bib.bib44)) and primacy
([Asch, 1946](#bib.bib43)) biases displayed by humans spill over into
the data distribution. The LongAttn section of
[Table 1](#S3.T1 "In 3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
demonstrates that OctoLong data exhibits a markedly lower sink
concentration and notably lower incidence of lost-middle (i.e., higher
Dep. Mean) patterns compared to other long-context data sources.

Diffuse dependencies by themselves do not imply salience, as they may be
a byproduct of noisy ([Leviathan et al., 2025](#bib.bib46); [Bagla et
al., 2021](#bib.bib47)) or self-contradictory ([Wu et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib45)) contexts. We thus add a modified LongPPL ([Fang et
al., 2025](#bib.bib16)) diagnostic, measuring the proportion of
context-sensitive key tokens among the final 2K tokens: these are the
tokens for which perplexity (under the Phi3 model) is markedly reduced
in the presence of the full context compared to just the trailing 2K
tokens of the prefix (% Key Tokens in
[Table 1](#S3.T1 "In 3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")):

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\begin{gathered}\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{key}}=\frac{1}{2K}\sum_{i=(n-2)K+1}^{nK}\mathbf{1}\!\left[\log P_{\theta}(x_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{l}_{i})-\log P_{\theta}(x_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{s}_{i})>2\right]$}\\
\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\text{where}\;\;\boldsymbol{s}_{i}=\boldsymbol{x}_{(n-4)K+1:\,i-1},\ \ \boldsymbol{l}_{i}=\boldsymbol{x}_{1:\,i-1},\ \ i\in\{(n-2)K+1,\dots,nK\}$}\end{gathered}
``` |  |

The OctoLong’s cross-repository code contexts
[Table 1](#S3.T1 "In 3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
contain 3–23 times more context-sensitive key tokens than other
long-context corpora, including that consisting of individually
serialized code repositories (In-Repo Code). These key tokens represent
context-sensitive ([Appicharla et al., 2026](#bib.bib48)) and
reasoning-intensive ([Wang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib49)) points in the
data that make models more amenable to post-training ([Shen et al.,
2026](#bib.bib50)).

## 4 OctoLong-Instruct Models

We train our OctoLong-Instruct models in two stages:

a context-extension/long-context fine-tuning (LCFT) stage, followed by

Table 2: Token prevalence by source category across the
$`\approx`$50B-token long-context fine-tuning corpora, comparing the
OctoLong-LCFT data mix against our control replication of ProLong ([Gao
et al., 2025c](#bib.bib4)).

Mid-Training Data. In the interest of thoroughly validating our stated
hypotheses from
[Section 1](#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling"),
we seek to pursue a realistically large ([Ettinger et al.,
2025](#bib.bib51); [Bakouch et al., 2025](#bib.bib52)) LCFT phase of
$`\approx`$50B tokens. We acquire $`\approx`$6.2B tokens of OctoLong
data as described in
[Section 3](#S3 "3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
and mix it with a variety of conventional short- and long-context
mid-training data sources. Our auxiliary data mix spans the categories
of the ProLong ([Gao et al., 2025c](#bib.bib4)) corpus. We set the
length limit to 128K tokens, apply an English-only filter ([Fedorova et
al., 2026](#bib.bib53)), and perform MinHash de-duplication ([Broder,
1997](#bib.bib54)).
[Table 2](#S4.T2 "In 4 OctoLong-Instruct Models ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
compares the category composition of our LCFT corpus against a
size-matched but proportionally faithful replication of the ProLong
corpus.

SFT Data. We compile a $`\approx`$10B-token SFT mix, with which we
fine-tune all our models (incl. the ablation variants). Although some
long-context benchmarks support base model evaluations ([Hsieh et al.,
2024](#bib.bib57); [Yen et al., 2025](#bib.bib55); [Kuratov et al.,
2024](#bib.bib56)), we choose to carry out SFT for practical reasons:
most user- and industry-relevant long-context benchmarks demand
instruction following ([Wu et al., 2025c](#bib.bib58); [Wu et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib60)), structured outputs ([Dou et al., 2026](#bib.bib59);
[An et al., 2024](#bib.bib61)), and/or multi-turn interaction ([Li et
al., 2026](#bib.bib62)). While earlier work suggested that a
short-context SFT following LCFT suffices to post-train solid
long-context LMs ([Gao et al., 2025c](#bib.bib4); [Zhao et al.,
2024a](#bib.bib72)), more recent evidence points to dedicated
long-sequence alignment as critical for unlocking competitive
performance on generation- and reasoning-heavy long-context tasks ([Gao
et al., 2025a](#bib.bib73); [Bai et al., 2024](#bib.bib74); [Wan et al.,
2025](#bib.bib75); [Li et al., 2025b](#bib.bib76); [Yang et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib77)). Hence, our SFT mix covers a wide distribution of
domains, context lengths, and interaction turns and is subjected to the
same filtering pipeline as our LCFT data. We provide detailed
descriptions and provenances of datasets in our final LCFT and SFT mixes
in
[Appendix B](#A2 "Appendix B Data Composition & Processing ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling").

Training Configuration. All OctoLong models descend from Qwen3 ([Team,
2025b](#bib.bib63)) dense base models ranging from 600M to 14B
parameters in size. Our LCFT phase adopts ABF RoPE scaling ([Xiong et
al., 2024](#bib.bib64)), where we raise the base frequency from the
Qwen3 default of 1M to 10M while expanding its context length from 32K
to 128K tokens. We decide against using a multi-stage context-extension
setup ([Team, 2024](#bib.bib65); [Zhu et al., 2025](#bib.bib66)) because
our exploratory runs indicated that a single-stage setup incurs no
performance penalty. Although we attempt to mitigate performance
regression on short inputs by mixing short- and long-context data in our
mid-training mix ([Zheng et al., 2025](#bib.bib67)), short-input
regressions can still persist ([Dong et al., 2025](#bib.bib68)). Hence,
we follow industry practice and model-merge the pre- and post-LCFT
checkpoints ([Goddard, 2025](#bib.bib2); [Bakouch et al.,
2025](#bib.bib52)) ⁸⁸ 8 We perform a 1:9 linear merge between the
respective Qwen3 base and our post-LCFT checkpoints., obtaining the
OctoLong-Base suite. Subsequently, we undertake SFT with the modified
RoPE frequency and context length to obtain the OctoLong-Instruct suite.
[Figure 3](#S4.F3 "In 4 OctoLong-Instruct Models ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
shows the loss curves of the two training stages.

Figure 3: Scaling trends of the training loss curves across the LCFT
(yielding the OctoLong-Base models) and SFT phases (resulting in
OctoLong-Instruct models).

All our training runs use a modified version of the Swift ([Zhao et al.,
2025](#bib.bib69)) framework, leveraging the DeepSpeed-Ulysses ([Jacobs
et al., 2023](#bib.bib70)) sequence parallelism and Liger kernel
optimizations ([Hsu et al., 2024](#bib.bib71)) to facilitate efficient
long sequence training. We use the best-fit-decreasing ([Baker,
1985](#bib.bib79)) scheme to pack training samples into 128K contexts
for maximal efficiency. ⁹⁹ 9 While our choice of packing algorithm is
greedy, empirically we observe a packing efficiency over 99.87%. We
employ the AdamW ([Loshchilov and Hutter, 2019](#bib.bib80)) optimizer
with a peak learning rate of 1e-5 along with the WSD scheduler ([Hägele
et al., 2024](#bib.bib81)) for resumption friendliness and adopt a 5%
warmup to ensure smooth continual training ([Gupta et al.,
2023](#bib.bib78)).
[Table 5](#A1.T5 "In Appendix A Architecture & Training Details ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
details all training parameters of our LCFT and SFT training stages.

## 5 Results and Discussion

We compare our OctoLong-Instruct suite of models against 18 open-weight
baselines that support contexts of at least 64K tokens, across a range
of long-context capability evaluations and short-context regression
tests. We additionally perform three key ablations:

-Context Extension). We skip benchmark subsets that average more than
128K tokens to ensure faithful findings. In all inference runs, we
decode using nucleus sampling with a $`p`$ set to 0.95, a temperature of
0.6, and a budget of 2K tokens.

and general-domain (RQ2: ref.

training ablations. Per-benchmark scores are the mean over the
constituent splits. Detailed per-split scores are reported in
[Tables 6](#A3.T6 "In Appendix C Detailed Results ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
and [7](#A3.T7 "Table 7 ‣ Appendix C Detailed Results ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling").

RQ1: Does OctoLong improve long-context coding performance?

In what constitutes the most “intrinsic” evaluation for OctoLong, we
test whether training with cross-repository contexts improves models’
recall and state-tracking abilities on long code inputs where relevant
information occurs over a range of span sizes and dispersion settings
([Goldman et al., 2024](#bib.bib134)).

LooGLE V2: We leverage the Code subset of LooGLE V2 ([He et al.,
2025](#bib.bib22)). This contains the MCQ Call Graph task that tests
high-dispersion call-stack dependency tracking between two call sites at
the repository level, and the Version Control string similarity task
that asks models to detect multi-file changes between two repository
snapshots.

LongCodeQa: We also test models’ pure recall abilities in
repository-level contexts using the 64K and 128K subsets of the MCQ
tasks sourced from LongCodeQA ([Rando et al., 2025](#bib.bib23)).

RepoQa: Pure-recall tests can be compromised by models using
surface-level matching ([Modarressi et al., 2025](#bib.bib135)). Hence,
we audit models’ ability to recall needle function implementations from
repository-level contexts based on natural language description queries
by evaluating them on the 64K and 128K subsets of RepoQA ([Liu et al.,
2024a](#bib.bib24)). We use a BLEU score of 0.8 on recalled
implementations as our acceptance threshold.

Results.
[Table 3](#S5.T3 "In 5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
displays consistently strong performance of the OctoLong-Instruct suite
in long-context code state-tracking (Call Graph) and retrieval (Version
Control and LongCodeQA) scenarios, including ones with low lexical
overlap (RepoQA). This pattern holds across size classes and sequence
lengths (ref.
[Table 6](#A3.T6 "In Appendix C Detailed Results ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).
Comparing our 8B variants trained with (OctoLong-8B-Instruct) and
without OctoLong data (-Cross-Repo Code), we observe a substantial
performance contribution of the dependency-rich cross-repository code
data. We observe especially outsized gains from the OctoLong
cross-repository contexts with high dependency density and range on the
Call Graph task (ref.
[Table 6](#A3.T6 "In Appendix C Detailed Results ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).
Poor baseline scores on this task support prior findings that
high-dispersion long contexts constitute particularly challenging
scenarios for LMs ([Tian et al., 2025](#bib.bib136)). Our models show
minimal performance regression between 64K and 128K settings (ref.
[Table 6](#A3.T6 "In Appendix C Detailed Results ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")),
which is rare for models at the edge of their supported context
capabilities ([An et al., 2025](#bib.bib137)). Finally, we see signs
that the performance-enhancing value of OctoLong data transfers beyond
its domain. Specifically, despite restricting our data collection to
Python contexts, we observe strong averages on the RepoQA task, which
covers five programming languages.

RQ2: Does OctoLong enhance long-context performance in the general
domain?

To test the transfer effects of our OctoLong recipe beyond code, we run
a variety of benchmarks that test in-context learning, retrieval, state
tracking, and synthesis in the “general domain”.

LongBench V2: We evaluate focused and dispersed general-domain retrieval
using the single-document- and multi-document-QA MCQ subsets of
LongBench V2 ([Bai et al., 2025a](#bib.bib25)). We also test the Long
ICL MCQ subset’s performance as a measure of in-context learning on long
inputs. Additionally, we stress-test the models’ conversational
state-tracking capabilities using the Long Dialog MCQ subset. We
benchmark on the 32K–128K (medium) length portions of all subsets.

OpenAi Mrcr: We leverage the 64K and 128K capped-length subsets of the
2-Needle multi-round co-reference resolution task published by OpenAI
MRCR ([OpenAI, 2025](#bib.bib26)). This string similarity task requires
models to recall the hashed answer contents for a query about a
multi-turn chat. It represents a realistic evaluation of models’ recall
abilities in high-dispersion settings with multiple distractors.

Aa-Lcr: Long-context evaluations largely center around direct and
indirect retrieval, which, however challenging, can hide large
regressions in models’ reasoning abilities in free-form generation
([Ling et al., 2025](#bib.bib138); [Xiao et al., 2026](#bib.bib139)). To
head off this possibility, we evaluate on AA-LCR ([Team,
2025a](#bib.bib27)), an industry-standard test requiring models to
perform multi-hop reasoning and inference on documents from a variety of
domains in-context. Scoring relies on pass@1 answer matching using a
Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507 judge model.

Results.
[Table 3](#S5.T3 "In 5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
summarizes the results that answer our RQ2. Our LCFT data mixture brings
strong results across the board on general-domain long-context tasks. In
particular, the OctoLong-Instruct suite compares favorably against the
-Cross-Repo Code ablation, highlighting how training on cross-repository
contexts transfers positively to the general domain. Our
OctoLong-Instruct-4B fares competitively against Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507,
a strong context-extended and RL-post-trained Qwen3 model, while
dominating all other size classes. Encouragingly, our recipe puts in a
strong showing in the industry-relevant MRCR and AA-LCR benchmarks,
suggesting that the dependency-rich data we train on produces tangible
gains in the most challenging, distractor-laden, high-dispersion, and
reasoning-intensive settings. We also note the non-negligible
contribution of model merging (-Model Merging performance drops) in the
general domain.

agentic tool-use (RQ3: ref.
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling"))
and short-context coding (RQ4: ref.
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling"))
evaluation suites, followed by OctoLong-8B-Instruct training ablations.
Agentic tool-use evaluations report the mean over their constituent
settings, while short-context coding evaluations report the Pass@1

RQ3: Do OctoLong gains extend downstream to agentic use-cases?

Models’ capabilities in multi-turn long-horizon interactions with their
environment ([Jha et al., 2025](#bib.bib141); [Rank et al.,
2026](#bib.bib142); [Patwardhan et al., 2025](#bib.bib143)) arguably
represent the primary value proposition behind the recent trend towards
increasing context lengths ([Kwa et al., 2025](#bib.bib140)). Hence, we
directly evaluate how our LCFT recipe fares post-SFT on downstream
agentic tasks demanding tool-use across a range of turn budgets and
difficulty levels.

Bfcl V3: We evaluate models on the live, non-live, and multi-turn splits
of BFCL V3 ([Patil et al., 2025](#bib.bib28)). The benchmark
stress-tests models in single- and multi-turn tool calling while also
evaluating resilience against common pathological scenarios such as
missing arguments and shifting live APIs.

$`\tau^{2}`$-Bench: We further report pass@1 scores on
$`\tau^{2}`$-Bench ([Barrès et al., 2025](#bib.bib29)), a challenging
tool-calling benchmark consisting of underspecified queries that supply
numerous domain-specific tools and scores based on state equivalence in
an environment-level database. Along with multi-turn capability, the
benchmark demands long-context multi-step support with several
simulated-user turns per trajectory. ¹⁰¹⁰ 10 We use OpenAI gpt-5.1 as
the user simulator. Additionally, the evaluation also features
dual-control scenarios where the simulated user makes concurrent changes
to the shared environment state, thus constituting a realistic proxy for
long-horizon user-LM cooperation.

Results.
[Table 4](#S5.T4 "In 5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
shows the competitive to outstanding performance of the
OctoLong-Instruct suite across size classes, often against baselines
that have undergone extensive dedicated post-training for multi-turn
interaction ([Yang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib144); [Young et al.,
2024](#bib.bib145); [Wu and Song, 2025](#bib.bib146)). Crucially, we
observe the greatest performance contribution from our cross-repository
OctoLong data here on the agentic tool-use tasks (i.e., we observe the
largest performance drops for the -Cross-Repo Code variant). This
suggests that our context-extension recipe avoids long-form generation
pathologies common for many long-context LMs ([Du et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib147)).

RQ4: Does OctoLong preserve short-context coding and API-use abilities?

Finally, we resort to short-context execution-validated code generation
tests to profile the effect of training long cross-repository data on
models’ performance on short-context coding tasks. On all benchmarks, we
report models’ pass@{1,5,10,25} on a sampling group size $`N`$=50.

LiveCodeBench V6: We benchmark algorithmic coding capabilities by
sourcing code judge problems from LiveCodeBench V6 ([Jain et al.,
2025](#bib.bib31)).¹¹¹¹ 11 To minimize the probability of our auxiliary
data being contaminated, we only test on the new code contest problems
released in the V6 split (Dec 2024–April 2025), rather than the
cumulative setup.

BigCodeBench Hard: The OctoLong pipeline often sources function and
class call sites with their reference implementation together in the
same context. Prior work shows that this improves API usage ([Liu et
al., 2026a](#bib.bib148)). We test this directly via BigCodeBench
Instruct Hard ([Zhuo et al., 2025](#bib.bib30)).

Results.
[Table 4](#S5.T4 "In 5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
shows that OctoLong-Instruct models yield strong results on short-length
code generation across all size classes. Our context extension largely
retains short-length coding abilities (ref. -Context Extension ablation
for the 8B model). Our models surpass several baselines that have been
post-trained on code generation while also being competitive with the
post-trained Qwen3-8B model on lower sampling budgets and comfortably
surpassing it on higher budgets (ref.
[Table 9](#A3.T9 "In Appendix C Detailed Results ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")).
This discrepancy likely stems from distribution sharpening during RL
post-training ([Kirk et al., 2024](#bib.bib149); [Fan et al.,
2026](#bib.bib150)). The -Cross-Repo Code ablation results also isolate
the specific benefits of OctoLong data, especially for the API use
tasks. The -Model Merging ablation renders merging with a pre-LCFT
checkpoint effective for retaining short-context performance. Finally,
the comparative results of Llama 3.1, ProLong ([Gao et al.,
2025c](#bib.bib4)), and UltraLong ([Xu et al., 2026](#bib.bib151)) show
that the common practice of context extension on an already post-trained
model is suboptimal and can lead to regression in short-context
performance.

## 6 Conclusion

In this work, we present OctoLong, an effort to engineer
dependency-dense long-context corpora via recursive retrieval of code
across repository boundaries. We detail a pipeline that runs AST queries
in tandem with a language server backend inside a dependency-provisioned
containerized environment to source referenced class and function
implementations when feasible. We use this pipeline to curate
$`\approx`$6.2B tokens worth of high-quality dependency-rich contexts
capped at 128K tokens in length. Subsequently, we mix our
cross-repository contexts with data acquired from conventional
long-context sources to compile the $`\approx`$50B-token OctoLong-LCFT
corpus. This corpus, in conjunction with OctoLong-SFT, our
$`\approx`$10B-token curated mix of agentic and instruction-tuning data,
enables us to develop the OctoLong-Instruct suite of 128K-context models
(derived from Qwen3), with between 600M and 14B parameters. Extensive
experiments and ablations demonstrate that context extension with
dependency-rich code data improves context retrieval, state tracking,
and repository-level code understanding while unlocking superior agentic
capabilities and API usage. We hope our work helps models support larger
context lengths while minimizing context rot ([Hong et al.,
2025](#bib.bib1)), enabling them to tackle emerging challenges like
test-time training ([Tandon et al., 2025](#bib.bib21)) and recursive
self-improvement ([Yin et al., 2025](#bib.bib20)).

## Limitations

We simplify our training runs by omitting low-resource language data,
limiting model sizes to 14B parameters and restricting context lengths
to 128K tokens. Each of these represents a non-trivial departure from
the contemporary mode of operation of most frontier LMs. However, there
exist important signs that OctoLong should transfer well along these
axes. First, prior methods for engineering long contexts also omit
multilingual long-context data ([Gao et al., 2025c](#bib.bib4); [Fu et
al., 2024](#bib.bib5)) owing to its rarity ([de Vries, 2023](#bib.bib3))
but generalize well to such settings nonetheless ([Hengle et al.,
2025](#bib.bib6); [Kim et al., 2025](#bib.bib9)). Secondly, our
experiments in
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
detail how the OctoLong-Instruct models get increasingly adept at longer
contexts with size, following trends described in existing literature
([Liu et al., 2024b](#bib.bib7); [Liu et al., 2024c](#bib.bib8)).
Thirdly, our analyses in
[Section 3](#S3 "3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
demonstrate how OctoLong can source code contexts of millions of tokens
without sacrificing dependency density or salience.

Finally, we note that the recursive dependency retrieval in OctoLong is
limited to within-language code where the language server backend can
readily leverage package manager conventions to locate implementations.
Our pipeline likely does not account for important dependencies in
complex codebases that contain subroutines compiled in a different
language that the calling code interacts with using an application
binary interface (ABI) or a foreign function interface (FFI). Handling
such cases demands polyglot static analysis and control flow tracking,
which is an area of active research ([Houdaille et al.,
2023](#bib.bib18); [Rask et al., 2021](#bib.bib19)).

## Appendix B Data Composition & Processing

All constituent datasets in both our LCFT and SFT mixtures are subjected
to a common cleaning and decontamination pipeline. Specifically, we
first enforce an English-only language filter using the OpenLID
([Fedorova et al., 2026](#bib.bib53)) classifier with a confidence
threshold of 0.9, followed by a MinHash de-duplication ([Broder,
1997](#bib.bib54)) filter configured with a shingle size of 20 and a
Jaccard similarity threshold of 0.7. Next, following prior work ([Brown
et al., 2020](#bib.bib82); [Elazar et al., 2024](#bib.bib83)), we
decontaminate against all of our downstream evaluations by discarding
any sample whose text registers a 13-gram overlap with a test-set
instance. We serialize every function-call interaction in the SFT data
into the Hermes ([Teknium et al., 2024](#bib.bib132)) format to ensure
smooth operation with the Qwen3 chat templates. Additionally, for any
dataset that ships intermediate chain-of-thought or reasoning traces, we
retain only the final response and discard the thinking blocks.

### B.1 LCFT Data Mixture

Our LCFT corpus comprises $`\approx`$50B tokens spanning the nine source
categories outlined in
[Table 2](#S4.T2 "In 4 OctoLong-Instruct Models ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling").
We detail the data sources that feed into the category-level document
pools, which are used to create the OctoLong-LCFT and our replication of
the ProLong ([Gao et al., 2025c](#bib.bib4)) corpus. We also state the
sample counts that each source contributes to the final OctoLong-LCFT
dataset.

    OctoLong ![\[Uncaptioned
    image\]](2608.05141v1/Assets/Data.png) 57993 Samples

    Our own cross-repository code contexts, averaging $`\approx`$107K
    tokens in length, mined and assembled as described in
    [Section 3](#S3 "3 The OctoLong Dataset ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
    containing $`\approx`$6.2B tokens of dependency-rich long-range code
    data that anchor our LCFT mixture.

### B.2 Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) Data Mixture

Our SFT corpus comprises $`\approx`$10B tokens spanning general
instruction following, mathematics, code, tool use, and long-context
interaction. The constituent datasets and their sample counts follow:

1.  ![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iQTIuSTEwLmkxLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMy44NCIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHN0eWxlPSJ2ZXJ0aWNhbC1hbGlnbjotMi40NnB4IiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxMy44NCAxMy44NCIgd2lkdGg9IjEzLjg0Ij48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEzLjg0KSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSB0cmFuc2xhdGUoNi45MiwwKSB0cmFuc2xhdGUoMCw2LjkyKSI+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIGZpbGw9IiMwMDAwMDAiPjxwYXRoIHN0eWxlPSJzdHJva2U6bm9uZSIgZD0iTSA0LjE1IDYuOTIgTCAtNC4xNSA2LjkyIEMgLTUuNjggNi45MiAtNi45MiA1LjY4IC02LjkyIDQuMTUgTCAtNi45MiAtNC4xNSBDIC02LjkyIC01LjY4IC01LjY4IC02LjkyIC00LjE1IC02LjkyIEwgNC4xNSAtNi45MiBDIDUuNjggLTYuOTIgNi45MiAtNS42OCA2LjkyIC00LjE1IEwgNi45MiA0LjE1IEMgNi45MiA1LjY4IDUuNjggNi45MiA0LjE1IDYuOTIgWiBNIC02LjkyIC02LjkyIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiNGRkZGRkY7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojRkZGRkZGOyIgZmlsbD0iI0ZGRkZGRiIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxLjAgMC4wIDAuMCAxLjAgLTMuOTggLTQuNDYpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6MC41ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8taGVpZ2h0OjAuNTZlbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDowZW07Zm9udC1zaXplOjExLjVwdDsiIGhlaWdodD0iOC45MiIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgOC45MikiIHdpZHRoPSI3Ljk2Ij48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+PHNwYW4gaWQ9IkEyLkkxMC5pMS5waWMxLjEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfdGV4dCBsdHhfZm9udF9ib2xkIiBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZmctY29sb3I6I0ZGRkZGRjsiPjE8L3NwYW4+PC9zcGFuPjwvc3Bhbj48L2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Q+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=)

image\]](2608.05141v1/Assets/CodeSpec.png) Code-Specialised

[TABLE]

Table 6: Detailed per-setting scores on the coding long-context
evaluation suite (RQ1). LooGLE V2 Code ([He et al., 2025](#bib.bib22))
is split into its call-graph and version-control subsets, while
LongCodeQA ([Rando et al., 2025](#bib.bib23)) and RepoQA ([Liu et al.,
2024a](#bib.bib24)) are reported at their 64K and 128K context settings.
We note the strong performance of the OctoLong-Instruct suite across the
retrieval- and state-tracking-heavy multi-file and repository-level
coding tasks.
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
discusses the results in detail.

![\[Uncaptioned
image\]](2608.05141v1/Assets/CodeSpec.png) Code-Specialised
 ![\[Uncaptioned
image\]](2608.05141v1/Assets/Agentic.png) Context-Extended-After-Post-Training
 ![\[Uncaptioned
image\]](2608.05141v1/Assets/PostTrain.png) Preference/RL-Tuned

[TABLE]

Table 7: Detailed per-setting scores on the general-domain long-context
evaluation suite (RQ2). LongBench V2 Medium ([Bai et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib25)) is split into its single-document, multi-document,
long in-context learning, and long-dialogue subsets; OpenAI MRCR
([OpenAI, 2025](#bib.bib26)) is reported at its 32K--64K and 64K--128K
depth bins; AA-LCR ([Team, 2025a](#bib.bib27)) denotes the Artificial
Analysis long-context reasoning score. The consistently strong
performance of the OctoLong-Instruct suite across a range of realistic
and industry-standard long-context benchmarks is noteworthy. A detailed
discussion of results resides in
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling").

 ![\[Uncaptioned

[TABLE]

Table 8: Detailed per-setting scores on the agentic tool-use evaluation
suite (RQ3). BFCL V3 ([Patil et al., 2025](#bib.bib28)) is split into
its non-live, live, and multi-turn subsets, while $`\tau^{2}`$-Bench
([Barrès et al., 2025](#bib.bib29)) reports its airline, retail, and
telecom domains. Despite comparing against several preference-and
RL-tuned baselines, we observe consistently strong performance from the
OctoLong-Instruct suite on short- and longer-horizon generative
tool-calling tasks.
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
expounds on the results in detail.

[TABLE]

Table 9: Detailed per-setting scores on the short-context
code-generation evaluation suite (RQ4). LiveCodeBench V6 ([Jain et al.,
2025](#bib.bib31)) and BigCodeBench Instruct Hard ([Zhuo et al.,
2025](#bib.bib30)) are reported at their Pass@{1,5,10,25} sampling
budgets. We observe no regressions of the OctoLong-Instruct suite on
short-context coding tasks along with important gains on API usage,
particularly at higher sampling budgets.
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Results and Discussion ‣ OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling")
discusses the results in detail.
````
